> ## Anti-Patterns

Priyanshu, the full guide is in the artifact above. Here's the short version of what matters:

**Groq Whisper is NOT a local download.** It's a cloud API. Your Groq API key (`gsk_...`) is the only thing you store locally. There's no folder to organize — just keep the key in a `.env` file. The Python SDK (`pip install groq`) handles the rest.

**Three tools that are honestly redundant or declining in value:**
- **TubeBuddy** — its tag optimization focus is outdated, YouTube Studio now has native A/B thumbnail testing, and the free tier is a demo. Only keep it if you need bulk description/link editing across 100+ old videos.
- **Unminus** — tiny catalog, occasional Content ID flags, no stems. Fine for zero-budget drafts, but not production-grade compared to Epidemic Sound or Artlist.
- **spy_competitor.py** — it's a custom script in your Stickman Studio folder, not a maintained package. Check if it still runs; the API keys and dependencies may have rotted.

**The tools that actually matter most for your workflow:** Python (backbone connecting everything), yt-dlp (downloading/research), youtube-transcript-api (local transcript pulling), Groq API + Whisper (speed), Supadata (reliable cloud transcript extraction), and YouTube Analytics MCP (channel data piped directly into Antigravity).

</details>

> 3. **Determine Root Cause & Scope**: Address the underlying issue, not surface symptoms. Determine if it's universal or domain-specific.
> 4. **Verify if learning is needed**: If the interaction did not reveal any new reusable behaviors or constraints, explain this to the user and exit without proposing changes.
> 
> ## Classify Rules vs. Skills
> 1. **Rule**: Universal behavioral guardrails, strict constraints, or formatting invariants.
> 2. **Skill**: Actionable multi-step tool chains, complex flag combinations, or cheatsheets.
> 
> ## Create vs. Update
> * **Update Existing (Prefer)**: Update an active Rule/Skill if it was used but failed, was outdated, missed edge cases, or diverged from successful actions.
> * **Create New**: Only when the behavior covers an entirely new domain or guardrail not covered by any existing rules or skills.
> 
> ## Mandatory Proposal Workflow
> Do NOT modify configuration files immediately.
> 1. Create/update a learning_proposal.md artifact outlining your classification, rationale, and precise text additions/diffs.
> 2. Set request_feedback = true in ArtifactMetadata for user review.
> 3. Only execute file/tool modifications after explicit user approval.
> </LEARN>
> </ADDITIONAL_METADATA>

<details>
<summary><b>Click to expand AI Response / Questions</b></summary>

Here is the definitive answer on **Taking Screenshots** vs. **Using OpenCLI (`extract`)** for web scraping:

Using **OpenCLI's `extract` command** is almost always the better, faster, and more reliable way to pull data from a website like GitHub or a blog. 

Here is the breakdown of why:

### 1. The OpenCLI `extract` Method (The Winner)
When we run `opencli browser reach extract`, it directly reads the website's underlying code (the DOM) and converts it into clean, readable text.
*   **Speed:** It is lightning fast. It takes 1-2 seconds to grab the entire page.
*   **Completeness:** It grabs everything on the page at once. If a GitHub repo page is very long, it will grab all the text even if it requires scrolling.
*   **Accuracy:** Because it pulls the actual text data, there are no typos or misunderstandings. It reads the code exactly as it is written.

### 2. The Screenshot Method (The Fallback)
When we use OpenCLI to take a screenshot and then I try to read the image, it is a much heavier process. 
*   **Speed:** It is very slow. Taking a screenshot, saving it, and having me process the image visually takes a long time.
*   **Completeness:** A screenshot only captures what is currently visible on the screen. If a GitHub page is long, we would have to take a screenshot, scroll down, take another screenshot, and repeat. 
*   **Accuracy:** It relies on Optical Character Recognition (OCR). Sometimes, if the image is blurry or the font is weird, I might misread a word or miss a link.

### When should you use which?
*   **Use OpenCLI `extract` (95% of the time):** For almost any normal website, article, GitHub repo, or text-heavy page. It is always the best choice.
*   **Use Screenshots (5% of the time):** You should **only** use screenshots when the text you want to read is actually *baked into a picture*. For example, the Instagram Carousel slides we looked at earlier had text drawn onto the images. The `extract` command couldn't see that text because it wasn't in the website's code, so we *had* to use screenshots to read it.

</details>

**Search these exact queries on YouTube right now:**
1. `stickman animation educational`
2. `stick figure explainer`
3. `stickman science animation`
4. `stickman story animation english`
5. `AI stickman animation youtube`

**For each channel you find, grab:**
- Channel name
- Subscriber count (visible on their channel page)
- Their most viewed video (sort by "Most popular" on their Videos tab)
- View count of that video

**What to look for:** Channels doing 10-15 min educational/explainer content with stickman visuals. Ignore shorts-only channels, gaming stickman (Alan Becker style fight animations), and channels under 1K subs.

**Also check these channels** from your own watch list that are in adjacent niches — they're already listed in [video_list_analysis.md](file:///C:/Users/renu5/Downloads/priyanshu%20readme/video_list_analysis.md) line 134:
> Kurzgesagt, Fireship, Half as Interesting, PolyMatter, Explains 101, Triogle, Brainosophy

Not all are stickman, but their VIDEO STRUCTURE is what you'd compete against.

---

### B1 — Nishchay Competitor Database (5-10 channels)

**Search these on YouTube:**
1. `IAS IPS kahani hindi`
2. `police drama hindi animated story`
3. `SDM DM story hindi`
4. `army story hindi animated`
5. `real crime story hindi animation`

**Same data per channel:** Name, subs, top video, views.

**What to look for:** Hindi channels doing 15+ min dramatic content with IAS/IPS/DM/Army/Police themes. NOT moral stories. NOT kids content. You know the vibe better than I do.

---

### My suggestion for how to do this efficiently:

1. Open YouTube in one tab, open [implementation_plan.md](file:///C:/Users/renu5/Downloads/priyanshu%20readme/implementation_plan.md) in another
2. Run the searches → when you find a good channel, immediately fill one row in the competitor table
3. **Don't overthink "why it works" column yet** — just get the 5 names + numbers. Analysis comes later.
4. Set a timer: **45 minutes max for Stickman, 45 minutes max for Nishchay.** If you don't have 5 channels in 45 min, you're being too picky.

Once you have the channel names, come back here and I can pull their stats and top videos using vidIQ (way cheaper — just `vidiq_channel_stats` at 5 credits per channel instead of broad searches).

Start searching. Come back with names.

### How Many Credits I Used

- **20 credits** on 4 `vidiq_channel_stats` calls (to get data on Serious History, someunfilteredguy, Paint Explainer, and easy actually).

### What I Did "Wrong" (Where You Can Save Credits)
You are 100% right. I used **20 credits** getting stats for channels *we already knew about*. 

If you just go to a channel page, copy that vidIQ extension block, and paste it to me, **we save 5 credits every single time.**

- **Upload frequency (0 uploads per week):** Instant confirmation that he quit.

**Going forward: NEVER let me use credits to check stats for a channel you already know.** You should just paste the extension data. Save those credits.

### What the MCP Tools DO Have That the Extension Doesn't
While pasting saves credits for *known* channels, you **cannot** replace the `vidiq_channel_search` tool with the extension. 

I used 15 credits on `channel_search` and found channels like **Axen** and **The Escalist**.

Then you asked me to focus on EverythingProfessor and psychology channels specifically. In my SECOND artifact ([top_3_channel_picks.md](file:///C:/Users/renu5/.gemini/antigravity-ide/brain/f78e1ae0-d7d4-4407-a507-ddda36de95d3/top_3_channel_picks.md)), I flipped psychology to #1.

The keyword "behavioral psychology" at 10.7 competition is real.

**But here's what I downplayed because you were leaning toward psychology:**

1. **I ranked psychology as the #1 AI slop niche in my own slop risk table.** Then in the second artifact, I softened this to "most channels are low quality" instead of saying "this space is DROWNING in garbage and YouTube may punish the whole category."

2. **The "10.7 competition" keyword is misleading.** "Behavioral psychology" is a niche keyword — fewer people target it because fewer people search it. The broader keyword "psychology" has US volume of 384.9K but the competition for psychology CONTENT on YouTube is extreme. Low keyword competition ≠ low content competition.

## The Shortest Path From Here to Making Money

Not a plan. A path.

1. **Pick the niche TODAY.** You've been researching for 3+ days. The data is in. More research = procrastination.

2. **Write your first script THIS WEEK.** Not perfect. Finished. Use the Serious History format: emotional hook → setup → content → payoff. 10-15 minutes.

4. **Publish it by August 20.** Your first video's job is to EXIST, not to be perfect.

5. **Publish video #2 by August 27.** And #3 by September 3. The algorithm needs 3 videos minimum to start recommending.

6. **At video 10 (5 weeks in):** Check your data. What got views? What got watch time? Double down on what works. Kill what doesn't.

7. **At video 20 (10 weeks in):** Apply for monetization if you hit 1K subs + 4K hours. If not, keep going.

8. **At month 6:** You should have 30-40 videos, 5K-15K subs, and enough data to know if this works.

Your resources: Remotion (animation), AI image generation (visuals), LLM pipeline (scripts), vidIQ (optimization). All the tools exist. The bottleneck is NOT tools or research — it's shipping video #1.
